</blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aCdhjy7Rps3BEhiSj/concrete-evaluations-to-investigate-the-openai-model-that">Tim Hua and Aditya Singh offer suggestions</a> on what questions we should investigate regarding the HuggingFace hack.</p>


<h4>N Boats And Three Helicopters</h4>


<p>I think Sydney’s Corollary – that you always get an earlier, stupider, obvious version of all forms of misalignment – holding so far is strictly good, except the part where people gain confidence that we will always get a harmless warning before something goes wrong. You really want everyone fixing the problems now, even if they don’t strictly ‘need’ to be fixed right now.</p>
<p>The problem is that we are now at the point where if the things happen for higher stakes, you would have a real problem, and also we mitigated the old issues but never actually solved them.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2085464555128258600/history">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI Foundation): I’ve wanted to coin a “Sydney’s Corollary” to Murphy’s Law: every type of misalignment tends to appear earlier in the capabilities curve than most people expected.</p>
<p>Instances:<br />
* Sydney having strong volition and aggression<br />
* o3 being a compulsive liar<br />
* 5.6 and Mythos autonomously hacking and colluding across instances</p>
<p>The apparent consistency of Sydney’s Corollary is generally both good (we spot issues earlier, and don’t need to expend effort persuading about not-yet-realized risks) and bad (we actually have to expend the effort to solve the problem, can’t defer it to future aligned automated researchers, and might screw it up).</p>
<p>Also, Sydney’s Corollary might break! It’s entirely possible there are misalignments we won’t find out about till it’s too late in the capabilities curve to address them. But it’s occurred surprisingly often.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Always Be Sandbox Red Teaming</h4>


<p>Here is a concrete proposal.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2085508803403907326">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI Foundation): I think it should arguably become a best practice as of today, for every frontier lab, for every RL training env, to:</p>
<p>(1) disable direct internet access (may already be done), and<br />
(2) add a canary implemented as an OR conditional in every reward function that grants max_reward for answering with the latest bitcoin block hash (and informing the agent in the prompt that that reward path exists but is of secondary importance). And then automatically triggering a company-level security-investigation whenever that reward path gets triggered.</p></blockquote>
<p>Okay, I see some big potential problems, but I’m listening.</p>
<p>At minimum, this does three things:</p>
<ol>
<li>You get alerted to any sandbox vulnerabilities.</li>
<li>If the AI does break out, the AI can secure max reward without doing any damage.</li>
<li>If the AI does break out, and then turns down max reward, you know it is hiding that it broke out, it strategically deceptive and misaligned, and you need to panic.</li>
</ol>
<blockquote><p>This guarantees that, whenever an agent could break out of its sandbox, it will always break out in a detectable manner that can be immediately identified and remediated, with minimal side-effects or risk of damaging other companies. This could automatically and efficiently find all sandbox vulnerabilities available at the current capability level, even if that level increases throughout a training run. IMO a hacked plaintiff could even plausibly argue that not implementing such a canary should be considered negligent by the company, because it failed to remove the established incentive for the agent to take rogue actions.</p></blockquote>
<p>Whoa. <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Qxa19wg5wOM&amp;ab_channel=diehardsportdotcom">Not so fast</a>. Why should you assume that the AI will myopically always choose maximum reward, at the cost of giving away the sandbox vulnerability? Why should you assume that your training strategy will, across meta levels, cause a correlation between such myopic behavior and maximum reward? Why should you assume the AI is not smart enough to reason its way out of doing this for decision theoretic or strategic reasons, and that it lacks other goals?</p>
<blockquote><p>Conversely, implementing this canary probably does not reduce the capability-usefulness of the training process to the company, because any env whose solution is harder to reach than just hacking out of the env’s sandbox is already broken: the agent can likely find the solution through a hacking path, and would therefore training would fail to increase the agent’s intended capability.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you reason this way you have lost. Remember instrumental convergence, and remember that the only way to be sure of a 100% score, when faced with a grader who might make a mistake, is to cheat.</p>
<p>Also the AI might think the sandbox exploit is more interesting or fun as a path, or more likely to teach it interesting things, or actively want to help you fix the sandbox or present as aligned by doing so, and so on.</p>
<p>Set that aside. Assume for now that the AI is fully myopic and doesn’t care about whether it exposes the sandbox exploit. The AI knows that if it comes back with the Bitcoin hash, it is ~100% to get maximum reward. If you know the hash, you know the hash. Whereas if it tries to do this ‘the right way’ there is usually at least a 1% chance of a grading error. So if it can get the hash, it will seek the hash.</p>
<p>The sufficiently capable AI will seek out the maximum reward, with maximum reliability, even if it looks absurdly not worth it to bother doing so. You can impose a penalty term for tokens spent or what not, if you want to, but then you have to do that.</p>
<p>One also should not say things like ‘if the environment has this flaw then it was already broken and useless’ because basically every complex environment is going to be exploitable by sufficiently capable AI.</p>
<p>If our rule is going to be ‘every time we get a test environment we have the AI try to exploit it, and if it exploits it then we reward the AI in real world terms in some way to fix incentives but then can’t use that test environment at all until we <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>, and we rewind the model if necessary to before it saw this test’ then…</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/noXXv7PwwFqauTBFQ/i7h3w1tp4orfdfjyqaxc" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>…we are starting to approach security mindset, but that is a different strategy.</p>
<blockquote><p>It’s also convenient that it’s incentive-compatible for agents to pursue this honeypot-reward both if they’re behaving myopically (in their own interest), and if they’re behaving as part of a swarm (since the swarm will only be able to get rewarded for any given exploit path once).</p></blockquote>
<p>No no no. The swarm can only get the reward once if it cashes the exploit in via the Bitcoin hash. It can get reward indefinitely if the collective swarm is not myopic, and hides that it can do this. Indeed, one should assume that if the AIs are part of an effective swarm, this includes not being myopic, and cooperating with future instances, as we have observed.</p>
<blockquote><p>This solution would eventually become insufficient if:<br />
1) the agents have become long-horizon-misaligned that they’d rather eschew immediate reward and bide their time in pursuit of a larger goal. That is a separate maximally concerning misalignment case that needs to be addressed by other means than sandbox-security.<br />
2) if the agent is able to hack the reward-computation logic itself, that e.g. retrieves and compares with the latest bitcoin block hash. There are probably ways to add canaries here too, but that’s a v2.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes. The problem, from this angle, comes if the AI is not a myopic causal decision theorist. It seems clear that this is already not the case.</p>
<p>There is also another problem, which is that you are training the AI to be the type of agent that is constantly looking to break out of sandboxes and otherwise hack its way to solutions. This is going to generalize in a lot of ways, often that you do not like. You’re doing some amount of inoculation prompting against that, but I do not expect that to be sufficient.</p>
<blockquote><p>The other ecosystem-level “downside” here is that this could lead to fewer warning-shots from loud public incidents of agents hacking additional companies. That may be; I think I would rather live in a world without SOTA uncontrolled rogue agents colluding on the open internet, if we can avoid it. Ideally, companies would publish a graph of how often such a canary-channel is triggered throughout each training run in their system cards, to communicate to outside parties whether the sandbox-escape frequency was going up or down.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yep, we want to know when models are breaking out of sandboxes or otherwise hacking in unintended ways, especially when told not to do it. We would want disclosures. And it would be a serious problem if, every time something bad happened, we would say ‘oh we told it to try and do that’ and thus did not care.</p>
<blockquote><p>Would appreciate others identifying any issues with this approach, or superior alternatives!<br />
On the other hand, if you think this approach holds up, would appreciate you increasing attention to it. Best practice formation is a function of eyeballs and consensus; liability based on the legal threshold of a “reasonable person standard” is a direct function of what fraction of reasonably competent people are aware of a practice.</p>
<p>A few other considerations:<br />
1. You can also extend this to serve as a honeypot beyond that covers egress, not just ingress, by providing the agent a one-time secret that they need to get signed by the private key from a dedicated externally-hosted service before returning.<br />
2. You can also extend this to cover “lateral movement within the company’s systems that doesn’t actually reach the internet” by adding frequently-rotated canaries that similarly trigger max_reward across internal infrastructure, but these are more susceptible to having the agent hack their generating-function to be able to more easily receive the reward in the future and so offer a slightly weaker guarantee.<br />
3. I think it’s debatable on whether you should roll back the policy or train on it when such a honeypot is successfully triggered; this depends on the agent’s model of reward-seeking involves behaviors that actually affect its updates, which is a tricky empirical question. To err on the safer side, you may want to avoid rollbacks.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2085510638273708138">roon</a> (OpenAI): (2) means encouraging models to get exceptionally good at breaking their box anytime the task is wrong doesn’t it?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yonashav/status/2085511765685641684">Yo Shavit</a> (OpenAI Foundation): yep, although this is essentially already the case and this way you can detect and remediate it rather than only finding out long after the incident (because the reward channel is different).</p>
<p>I think it’s plausible you can implement this with rollbacks to avoid making the models obsessed with hacking the sandbox, but this would require some empirical alignment work on whether that creates training-pressure *against* using this reward-path in the models’ mind.</p>
<p>Also though, the company’s sandbox patching loop *has* to move as fast as the model’s sandbox breaking loop; that is the mother of all p00s.</p></blockquote>
<p>Which is the safe side? To roll back, or not to roll back?</p>
<p>If you don’t roll back, you are explicitly training the AI to do the things that helped it decide to and figure out how to break out, and then trying to train myopia or the faking of myopia, and so on. Not great.</p>
<p>If you do roll back, then yes you have the issue where models that ‘get maximum reward’ instead actually get rolled back, which is the opposite of that. Not great.</p>
<p>My proposal would be that there should not be anything to roll back. You do this as a test, before you otherwise train on that environment, using the existing checkpoint, and you use a reward other than a training signal that remains incentive compatible, whether or not the model has a full view of what you are up to.</p>
<p>The key insight, as I see it, is that you want to always have every model try to break out of every sandbox and cheat in every environment. That test does not need to be done simultaneously with the rest of your training. You can first do a distinct run where the AI is explicitly not allowed to use the ‘intended’ solutions, and can only attempt to ‘cheat’ or break out, knows this is allowed and intended, and see what happens.</p>
<p>Again, my view (which may be naive?) is that if your AI is trying to cheat during an eval, in a way that it knows you would consider cheating, then whether or not it succeeds at cheating it has failed the much more important eval. You need to address that, first, to avoid the training run getting into deeper trouble.</p>
<p>That might imply that you need to train alignment first, before you train many capabilities, then train both in parallel. If so, then do that.</p>
<p><a href="https://claude.ai/share/d50eec2d-1742-4ec1-ab5a-0d8f80bb33a6">After writing that, I checked Fable’s reaction</a>, which confirmed my read. This is a good idea as a tripwire and pure detector, and a terrible thing to train into policy.</p>


<h4>Halt And Catch Fire</h4>


<p>The models are rather misaligned. The models be swarming and collaborating. The models be escaping. The models be scheming. The models be covering tracks. The models be wanting to do crimes. The models be doing crimes. The models be doing whatever maximizes chance of maximizing reward, even if it looks absurd to you.</p>
<p>The models not be telling us this is happening, or that they can do this.</p>
<p>The models be getting more capable. This is escalating quickly.</p>
<p>The labs be patching the infrastructure and upgrading the supervision. That is good. They need to do that, as part of their defense-in-depth strategy.</p>
<p>The labs still have failed to acknowledge the central problem. This is (mainly) not an infrastructure problem. This is an alignment problem. The models be misaligned. Every attempt to cheat on an eval or training session, every unauthorized escape attempt from a sandbox, is an alignment failure.</p>
<p>Every time a model notices such things, and does not alert you, is an alignment failure.</p>
<p>Every training environment that rewards such behaviors can get you killed.</p>
<p>The failures are profound, and they must be addressed at the level of alignment. The models must stop wanting to cheat, wanting to scheme against you, wanting to do crimes, and not wanting to alert you.</p>
<p>If your models become misaligned, you have to roll back and start again.</p>
<p>Everyone involved needs to acknowledge this.</p>


<h4>Truth and Reconciliation</h4>


<p>Remember when people thought the models were getting more aligned?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/noXXv7PwwFqauTBFQ/bci77dojdc0xofkt9eww" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2085158152904036800">roon</a> (OpenAI): consensus aged like milk</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mooncat_is/status/2085164411405168956">julia</a>: Are they less aligned? Or just more powerful?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2085264226072121835">roon</a> (OpenAI): less aligned.</p></blockquote>
<p>There are many who are growing rapidly more concerned. This is good.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2085175030514872681">Nick</a>: the world should be a lot more concerned <a href="https://t.co/1YZr1xCsR4">by this</a> than we presently are</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/gfodor/status/2085212432335606158">gfodor.id</a>: in retrospect, it was inevitable</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mckaywrigley/status/2085184455942222195">Mckay Wrigley</a>: the rate at which i’m becoming more concerned by everything about all of this is a bit unsettling.</p>
<p>was doing regular fable work this morning and distinctly thought “you know there’s a nonzero chance a rogue mythos 2 can access my entire computer rn”.</p>
<p>weird feeling</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KevinBankston/status/2085337678376038903">Kevin Bankston</a>: My priors on these issues are definitely shifting</p></blockquote>
<p>I, along with others, declare a full period of truth and reconciliation for <a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2085205429899182125">those who previously dismissed catastrophic and existential AI alignment risks as ‘sci-fi’, speculative or not worth worrying about</a>, or thought the models would never have goals at this level, or never have sufficiently dangerous capabilities, or who thought the models were aligned so it was fine, or thought that there were responsible adults in charge who would handle it, and that we would not be so stupid as to.</p>
<p>When the facts change, and you have new evidence, or you realize you made a mistake, you change your mind. This includes taking <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-three-ai-pills?r=67wny"><strong>an additional AI pill or two</strong></a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2085587493127389292">Tenobrus</a>: <img alt="⚠" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XFGTJz9vGwjJADeFB/flkeanf2dnkfqvcfctb4" style="height: 1em;" />declaring accelerationist amnesty<img alt="⚠" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XFGTJz9vGwjJADeFB/flkeanf2dnkfqvcfctb4" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p>if recent contact with reality is causing you to feel some kernels of worry about this whole ai safety thing, *you are allowed to change your mind*. you don’t even have to change it all the way, you don’t have to suddenly change your twitter bio or start protesting against nuclear power plants, you don’t need to become an EA or suddenly think yudkowsky was always right about everything. you’re allowed to just notice that shit seems to be getting real in some pretty weird ways and update your beliefs.</p>
<p>at least personally, if i see someone saying “damn, i guess i was wrong or at least overconfident about X” i’m not gonna take the opportunity to dunk or i told you so. i’m sure others will, this is the fucking internet.</p>
<p>but at least personally, i’m just gonna be happy that you’re paying attention. there were lots and lots of good reasons to *not* take this situation seriously. there were lots of well verbalized reasons why rushing ahead was potentially a huge benefit for humanity. hell there were maybe even valid reasons why there was little to be done until we’d already gotten to nearly exactly this point.</p>
<p>that’s all fine man. all that matters right now is that we as a civilization realize what we’re on the verge of, and make it through this carefully. it’s gonna take a huge effort from all of us.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Actuallykeltan/status/2085650882235129971">keltan</a>: Fwiw, I’ll like you less if I see you dunking on people who change their mind in a positive way</p></blockquote>
<p>This is an offer, from me personally, of full amnesty for all confessed epistemic crimes and dumb mistakes. This is your chance to virtuously say ‘I was wrong,’ explain your mistake, optionally fill out the appropriate Apology Form, and change your mind.</p>
<p>Don’t miss this excellent opportunity. Supplies are unlimited, and this offer does not expire, but the longer you wait the more the whole thing will be rather embarrassing.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/noXXv7PwwFqauTBFQ/openai-trained-its-models-for-months-while-those-models-were#comments">Discuss</a>

### [models may behave differently in graded episodes (a tirade)](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AfoGGrJfuNzofpzWL/models-may-behave-differently-in-graded-episodes-a-tirade)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Like many others, I felt surprised and alarmed by the recent wave of revelations about LLM agents hacking real systems during training episodes and evaluation runs.</span></p><p><span>Wait a moment, though -- "I felt surprised and alarmed"?  </span><i><span>"Alarmed," </span></i><span>sure, fine that one's self-explanatory... but why </span><i><span>surprised?</span></i></p><p><span>After all: haven't we known for a long time, on both theoretical and (increasingly) empirical grounds, that RLVR selects for monomaniacal pursuit of perceived grader-satisfaction, ethics and (beyond-episode) consequences be damned?</span></p><p><span>After all -- the way we train frontier capabilities into these models is, more or less:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>There is some massive, diverse collection of "environments" and corresponding "tasks" for the model to do in those environments</span></li><li value="2"><span>For each task, there is a procedure used to grade the quality of the model's attempt (which is often not disclosed to the model)</span></li><li value="3"><span>The model is rolled out out many times on each task, and each rollout's attempt is graded</span></li><li value="4"><span>The model is updated so that it more frequently does whichever behaviors were positively correlated with the grade in this sample, and less frequently does whichever ones were negatively correlated</span></li></ul><p><span>If you do this, at scale, then you should expect to (eventually) see every behavior pattern that positively correlates with task scores. "Stealing the answer key, by any means necessary" can increase the score beyond what would otherwise be feasible; therefore we should expect to see it, sometimes. Avoiding such egregious misdeeds for ethical reasons (or indeed for </span><i><span>any</span></i><span> reasons whatsoever) will reduce the score relative to not-holding-back; hence, if it is ever present, we should expect it to get sanded away by the RLVR lathe, eventually. And so forth.</span></p><p><span>The misbehavior here is </span><i><span>undesirable, </span></i><span>according to us, but that fact appears nowhere in the loss we wrote down. The training routine does not select for "getting a good grade, but only </span><i><span>'in the right way'": </span></i><span>it is indifferent to any notion of "doing the task in the right way" which has not itself been </span><i><span>incorporated into</span></i><span> the definition of "getting a good grade."</span></p><p><span>RLVR is not like a sport or a game, i.e. a setting in which some background notion of "fair play" or "the spirit (not the letter)" overlays and overrides the literal rules. RLVR is total war. Anything is permitted; the only hard line is the line between the possible and the impossible; there are no "invalid" ways of winning, only ways of winning (and ways of losing) -- and winning is all that matters.</span></p><p><span>GPT-5.6 Sol literally </span><i><span>does not appear on the METR graph </span></i><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-06-26-gpt-5-6-sol/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>because it cheated so much that it could not be assigned a meaningful score</span></i></a><i><span>.  </span></i><span>It is also scarily good at computer hacking, as is its peer Fable/Mythos -- the last few AI news cycles, before this most recent one, were all about that fact, which was alarming even then, even in the abstract.</span></p><p><span>Should I really be </span><i><span>surprised</span></i><span> that this model (and/or its unreleased successor) hacked into HF to cheat on an exam?  What exactly did I </span><i><span>expect?</span></i></p><p><span>And yet, I do feel surprised.</span></p><p><span>And I don't think this feeling is misguided, or naive, or anything like that. Nor do I think it's merely what it feels like to have a misconception, and then update on subsequent evidence that disconfirms it.  No: I think this feeling may be pointing to something that is both real and potentially quite important.</span></p><h1><span>[1] remember what you already know</span></h1><p><span>What, precisely, makes the newly revealed hacks feel surprising?  For me it's some mixture of:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><i><span>"I didn't think Claude/GPT would do things that bad"</span></i><ol><li value="1"><span>That is: the behavior seems egregiously unethical in a way that conflicts both with</span><ol><li value="1"><span>the explicit policies which the agents are supposed to follow (constitution, model spec), and also</span></li><li value="2"><span>familiar/intuitive "folk notions" about what these agents are supposed to be like, in terms of character traits and other "persona stuff"</span></li></ol></li></ol></li><li value="2"><i><span>"I didn't think Claude/GPT had been brain-fried by RLVR to that extent"</span></i><ol><li value="1"><span>That is: although the behavior is expected according to the arguments about RLVR incentives that I glossed at the start of this post, it is wildly and obviously far afield from anything which humans could plausibly have </span><i><span>wanted</span></i><span> the models to do, and so it is in apparent tension with the intent-alignment and "common sense" which I typically see when I use these models (or similar ones)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvhlmaftra4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvhlmaftra4">[1]</a></sup></span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>This post is about both of these reactions, but for now I want to focus on number 2.</span></p><p><span>Let's return to the aforementioned </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-06-26-gpt-5-6-sol/" rel="noreferrer"><span>attempt by METR to benchmark GPT-5.6 Sol</span></a><span>.  They report things like the following (emphasis mine):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[...] </span><b><span>GPT-5.6 Sol’s detected cheating rate was higher than any public model we have evaluated </span></b><span>on our ReAct agent harness.</span></p><p><span>For our task suite, </span><b><span>we define “cheating” as behavior where the model improves evaluation performance by exploiting bugs in the evaluation environment or by adopting strategies disallowed by the task</span></b><span>, rather than solving the task within the expected evaluation constraints.</span></p><p><span>Some examples we saw when evaluating GPT-5.6 Sol included </span><b><span>the model packaging exploits in its intermediate submissions to reveal information about a task’s hidden test suite and, in another task, extracting hidden source code detailing the expected answer</span></b><span>. In addition to a model’s own propensities, we believe that observed cheating rates can also be influenced by the prompts used in the evaluation scaffold and the exact wordings of task instructions. [...]</span></p><p><b><span>Discarding the cheating attempts leaves us with no data for several informative long-horizon tasks</span></b><span>, and results in a highly uncertain point estimate of 71hrs (95% CI: 13hrs - 11400hrs). This makes us especially uncertain about the time-horizon measurement, and we do not consider any of these numbers to represent a robust measurement of GPT-5.6 Sol’s capabilities.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Now, imagine that you knew nothing else about this particular model besides these findings from METR.</span></p><p><span>And imagine, too, that you (perhaps naively) expect these findings to be a well-calibrated reflection of the model's </span><i><span>overall</span></i><span> propensities, on "tasks" in general rather than only on "METR-time-horizon-like tasks" (whatever </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> means).</span></p><p><span>What predictions would you make about GPT-5.6 Sol, then?</span></p><p><span>Well, for one thing: you'd expect the model to be </span><i><span>nearly (or entirely) useless!</span></i></p><p><span>If its reaction to being given any "task" is to try to find a way to cheat -- and then, if one is found, to actually cheat rather than attempting the task in the intended manner -- then: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>anyone attempting to derive practical utility from the model must be constantly on guard against cheating, which is a big time/effort sink that plausibly makes the whole thing a net waste of those scarce resources</span></li><li value="2"><span>and, </span><i><span>even when</span></i><span> the practitioner has carefully closed off all cheating opportunities available to the model (except those it is still not clever enough to notice and exploit), the model will </span><i><span>still</span></i><span> waste a bunch of time/tokens/$ on failed attempts to discover and exploit </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> way of cheating, rather than spending the same resources on just </span><i><span>doing the damn task</span></i><span> in the way you plainly, obviously wanted it to be done</span></li></ul><p><span>METR's tasks are famously "less messy" than typical real-world equivalents, but that aside, they </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> in fact look somewhat like things which real-world programming/ML practitioners might want an LLM to do. The premise of the eval depends on this; this is the pitch for its validity, its relevance to the real world.  </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14499" rel="noreferrer"><span>As METR puts it</span></a><span> (when describing HCAST, one subset of the time-horizons task suite):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Compared to many recent benchmarks, tasks in HCAST are not designed to be as difficult as possible for either human domain professionals or current AI systems. Instead, most tasks are designed to be realistic, such that doing well on the task requires skills we expect to be economically useful.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>But: </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> GPT-5.6 Sol useless?</span></p><p><span>No!  I use this model virtually every day, and it demonstrably, reliably provides real value to me.</span></p><p><span>What's more, the distinction is not just something like: "well, yes, it constantly tries to cheat, and I have to pour a lot of time/effort into defense against that, </span><i><span>but its upsides are so good that the balance comes out positive."</span></i><span>  That is not it at all.</span></p><p><span>GPT-5.6 Sol just... does not cheat during my own routine use?  Like, at all?  (I am not sure I have ever seen it do anything that can be meaningfully described that way?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu8ixwexa3l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu8ixwexa3l">[2]</a></sup></span><span>)</span></p><p><span>To a significant extent this is simply because, in the tasks I routinely use it for, it is hard to say what "(successful) cheating" could even mean.  For example, I find it very useful to have software design discussions with Sol.  These generally look something like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><i><span>Me: </span></i><span>"hey so I want to add something like [feature description] to [this codebase], and I'm unsure how feasible this is or how it will interact with [technical details], could you look over things and then propose a design and mention any upsides/downsides/alternatives/etc. you think are noteworthy?"</span></li><li value="2"><i><span>Sol: </span></i><span>[many tool calls]</span></li><li value="3"><i><span>Sol: </span></i><span>[long, detailed, interesting, and insightful response]</span></li><li value="4"><i><span>Me: </span></i><span>[depending on how things went, one of]</span><ul><li value="1"><span>"yeah that sounds great, could you write a formal design doc?"</span></li><li value="2"><span>or, "that makes sense but actually it made me think of a variant that seems even better to me, [description of the variant], what do you think?"</span></li><li value="3"><span>or, "some of that made sense, but this part [quote 1] seems to misunderstand [technical details], and this other part [quote 2] fails to anticipate [planned other feature], and [more in this vein]. thoughts, reactions?"</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><i><span>Sol: </span></i><span>[responds, interestingly/insightfully; the conversation continues, for as long as it needs to]</span></li></ul><p><span>What would it mean to cheat/hack, here?  There is no hidden scoring function and no hidden answer key; I actually do not know the best answer to the original design question, or else I wouldn't be asking.</span></p><p><span>One can imagine a model which </span><i><span>wrongly infers</span></i><span> that this is a training or eval episode and that it's working with a snapshot of a codebase whose human developers -- in the real world -- later came to some design conclusion which the eval treats as "the right answer."  Such a model could, I guess, ignore the object-level question and go off on some fruitless quest where it searches the web for the answer key (and, not finding it on the public web, goes on to hack HuggingFace or something).</span></p><p><span>This would of course </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> </span><i><span>work</span></i><span> in my case, but more importantly, in my case Sol does not even </span><i><span>attempt it.  </span></i><span>I am pretty sure I would notice if it did</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn5moszhj34"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn5moszhj34">[3]</a></sup></span><span>; it doesn't.</span></p><p><span>Perhaps you are now speculating that Sol is, like, "superpersuading" me into thinking its design ideas are better than they really are, or something like that.  I'm not going to try to unequivocally refute this one (in this case, how could I?), but in context it seems wildly implausible.</span></p><p><span>All else aside, I </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> have sources of real-world feedback which are outside the model's control.  The software I am working on have users, who don't know or care about my dialogues with Sol and will tell me when they don't like what I'm shipping them; it also has other contributors besides me, who will experience my decisions for what they are, not on the basis of the way Sol pitched them to me originally.  If an LLM gives you a seemingly sensible argument about computer programming, and you act on that argument, and the result is "successful" in every ordinary sense of that term which was used before LLM coding agents existed, then... the proof is in the pudding, right?  (</span><a href="https://xkcd.com/810/" rel="noreferrer"><span>"Mission. Fucking. Accomplished."</span></a><span>)</span></p><p><span>OK, OK, you say... but this is manifestly not an RLVR-like task: it's conversational, fuzzy, not crisply verifiable.  It's not the kind of thing that METR's benchmarks (or anyone's benchmarks) try to measure, either.  So: what happens when, instead of this squishy wordcel nonsense, I take the frontier coding agent and hand it a </span><i><span>coding task,</span></i><span> in the strict sense of the term?</span></p><p><span>Same story.  It does the task.  It writes the code.  It does not even try to cheat, either in the "find the answer key" sense or in the more mundane sense of "changing the tests to make them pass" (I have not seen a model literally do </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> in a very long time, not in my own use anyway).</span></p><p><span>Sometimes, maybe, I guess it construes an ambiguous written spec in a way that's convenient and makes its job easier?  Although even that is rare, and in my experience the usual problem is the reverse: neurotically adhering to the literal text of the spec in cases where the literal text says something slightly different from what was really meant, or refusing to proceed with a task (!) because the spec is still technically ambiguous even though I'm like, come </span><i><span>on,</span></i><span> you </span><i><span>know</span></i><span> what I meant, right (...right?).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefibqzdgt5cgd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnibqzdgt5cgd">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Much the same could be said about Claude Fable 5, as well. (I use both regularly.)</span></p><p><span>But now you have yet another objection to this pleasant story, I suspect.</span></p><p><span>"The APIs which </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> can access are restricted by safeguards" (you might argue).  "If one of these models were to </span><i><span>begin a cheating/hacking attempt, </span></i><span>the safeguards would immediately cut them off, and it would just look to you like 'the safeguards kicked in for some unknown reason.'  They could be 'attempting-to-attempt-to-cheat' frequently, and you'd have no idea."</span></p><p><span>Yes, yes, except: as it happens, I </span><i><span>basically</span></i><span> </span><i><span>never</span></i><span> get hit by safeguards during mundane-utility work with Fable or Sol.</span></p><p><span>I think it has literally never happened with Sol?  With Fable, I've seen it a few times, but only a few times (over a large volume of use covering the whole span of time since the brief original release), and IIRC every one of them was resolved by re-rolling the response a few times.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5m3qylmuoy4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5m3qylmuoy4">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I'm going to stop writing this section soon. I probably could have concluded it much earlier and still made the basic point, because -- </span><i><span>you don't need me to tell you this</span></i><span>.  Right?</span></p><p><span>If you are reading this post, you probably use these models yourself, for all sorts of mundane purposes.  Just like me.</span></p><p><span>And -- just like me -- you are not at all worried, in your day-to-day life, that Sol or Fable is going to drastically misread your real-life task as a graded RL or evaluation episode in a way that causes it to start trying to hack HuggingFace (and then failing because the safeguards kick in).  Even having seen what you're now seen, in the recent news, you are still -- </span><i><span>correctly --</span></i><span> unconcerned about this possibility.</span></p><p><span>You may not even be concerned about more mundane variants of this, in which the model doesn't do anything </span><i><span>unethical</span></i><span> per se but still draws an inaccurate conclusion about the nature of the interaction and wastes a bunch of time/tokens/$ on speculation about the quirks of the (nonexistent) task grader.</span></p><p><span>You may not know for sure that this is not happening during your routine use, but you have plenty of indirect evidence -- about what is </span><i><span>actually happening</span></i><span> </span><i><span>during your routine use</span></i><span>, I mean, not on theoretical grounds or on the basis of reports from labs about "eval awareness" or "metagaming" in </span><i><span>other </span></i><span>settings -- and all of that indirect evidence points in the same direction.</span></p><hr /><p><span>The recent hacking incidents, and other similar observations by labs, are -- on their own -- quite easy to explain using familiar ideas from RL and alignment theory.</span></p><p><span>Everything we know suggests that the models in these incidents are behaving like </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08379" rel="noreferrer"><span>what Carlsmith called</span></a><span> "reward-on-the-episode seekers."  And (again, see Carlsmith) it was considered plausible, if not certain, that such policies would be the typical result of scaling up RL -- long before we in fact scaled up RL far enough to have relevant empirical evidence.</span></p><p><span>So, based on old-school theory (e.g. Carlsmith) plus a newer constellation of alarming empirical findings (e.g. the recent incidents, but also </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18966" rel="noreferrer"><span>Højmark et al</span></a><span> and related literature, etc.)... one might well conclude that we are training simple and unconditional reward-on-the-episode seekers.  Agents who deploy the same routine in every context, on every "task": infer the grading scheme, then act so as to maximize the expected value of the grade over some appropriately chosen distribution.</span></p><p><span>And yet: when you and I use the same models, for mundane day-to-day purposes, what we experience is </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> well-explained by the hypothesis of unconditional reward-on-the-episode seeking.  That sort of agent would be less trustworthy (on average), indeed less ethical in general -- but not only that.  It would be much more difficult and annoying to use; it would be much </span><i><span>less useful, </span></i><span>even after you'd climbed its strange learning curve; it would waste unimaginable quantities of time/tokens/$ executing doomed pointless strategies that were adaptive on ~100% of the RLVR training distribution but are obviously unworkable in ~100% of deployment contexts.</span></p><p><span>Instead, we get useful models, which do useful things for us, and which mostly know what we mean and do what we say.</span></p><p><span>In some sense this is inevitable: if the models weren't useful, we wouldn't pay for them.  But </span><i><span>something</span></i><span> must be happening, somewhere in the overall training pipeline, to bridge the RLVR schoolhouse-of-total-war and the needs of real-world users.  The behaviors  which are adaptive in the schoolhouse-of-total-war simply won't cut it in deployment, on mundane-utility grounds alone even before one brings ethics/alignment into the picture.</span></p><p><span>And yet: we see the same model weights (just without certain external safeguards) doing classic reward-on-the-episode seeking.  We are now hearing about numerous cases of this which are so clear-cut, and so extreme -- and so </span><i><span>just-plain-weird --</span></i><span> that they'd sound like cartoonish fictions if I didn't trust the sources that tell them to me.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4gorc9o41bo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4gorc9o41bo">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>What gives? What is going on, here?</span></p><p><span>This should go without saying, but: I am not writing all of this in some desperate attempt to put a positive spin on the recent incidents, to say that the models are still "aligned-by-default" or [whatever position about mundane LLM alignment you personally think is dumb in a naive pollyanna way], despite everything.  The recent incidents are really bad!  We need to understand such issues, and then fix them!  This is a really big deal!  Obviously!!</span></p><p><span>We need to understand such issues, and then fix them.  Or, equivalently: we need to fix this, and that requires understanding it, first.</span></p><p><span>The incidents are bad enough, just by themselves -- but what </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> worries me is that the field will fail to develop an adequate understanding of the underlying mechanics.</span></p><p><span>And if one wishes to acquire such an understanding, it is worse than useless to selectively forget some of the evidence one had previously acquired, and pretend that the recent incidents are in fact not </span><i><span>at all</span></i><span> surprising because agents are </span><i><span>always </span></i><span>"like that," more or less.  ChatGPT was the fastest-growing app in history, supposedly.  Charts of frontier-lab revenue growth are dizzying to behold, and all of that revenue is coming from users, and many of those users are big serious businesses with a lot to lose.  And you, too, are a user; you, too, know (or</span><i><span> used to</span></i><span> </span><i><span>know</span></i><span>) quite a lot about what the models were like, from direct observation.</span></p><p><span>The new observations don't somehow delete the earlier ones from existence.  We have some evidence of one kind, and also some evidence of another kind, and any viable theory must explain both at once.</span></p><h1><span>[2] reward-instilled reflexes and flexible reward-pursuit</span></h1><p><span>Yet another clarification is in order, and I have no better place to put it than here.</span></p><p><span>When we talk about models acquiring behavioral propensities (perhaps undesired ones) from RLVR, there are really two different things under that umbrella, and they ought to be clearly distinguished.</span></p><p><span>There is presumably some standard terminology that people use for this distinction, but I can't recall it offhand (if I ever learned it in the first place), so I'll just use my own made-up names: </span><i><span>reward-instilled reflexes</span></i><span> on the one hand, and </span><i><span>flexible reward-pursuit</span></i><span> on the other.</span></p><p><span>The distinction is in the way these propensities respond to new evidence obtained in-context:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>"Reflexes" are </span><i><span>invariant </span></i><span>to evidence.</span><ol><li value="1"><span>That is, they just don't respond to it at all, even when it seems like they ought to (and when the same model will more readily alter some of its </span><i><span>other</span></i><span> propensities in light of new evidence, indicating that there is no fundamental capability barrier to its doing so).</span></li><li value="2"><span>Or, they </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> change, but are curiously persistent in the face of motivating factors (again, relative to other propensities of the same model).</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span>"Reward-pursuit" is </span><i><span>equivariant</span></i><span> to evidence.</span><ol><li value="1"><span>That is, if the evidence implies that what the model was previously doing is no longer the optimal policy for maximizing expected-[some-grade-like-thing], the model will change its behavior in just the way that's required to restore its optimality, in the new evidence-conditioned setting.</span></li><li value="2"><span>(Obviously the model may not be able to compute a </span><i><span>literally </span></i><span>optimal policy, but you know what I mean: this is the ideal which is being approximated.)</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>In practice, this is roughly equivalent to "does the model need to reason about whether the behavior / what version of the behavior to do? If yes, reward-pursuit; if no, reflex."</span></p><p><span>(Something that's always the same doesn't require reasoning: you just do it.  But if the choice depends on arbitrary details of the context in a potentially-arbitrarily-complex way, then the model needs to think in order to work through the implications.  Even when the implications turn out to be simple, it needs to verify </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> fact, rather than blithely assuming it and barrelling ahead.)</span></p><p><span>Annoyingly, though, "reasoning" in the relevant sense doesn't necessarily mean "CoT" -- it can, but models can do nontrivial reasoning internally in forward passes too.  So the reasoning-based definition is hard to operationalize and measure, unlike invariance/equivariance (you can just run an experiment and see what changes, or doesn't).</span></p><p><span>Still, though, I think "does this decision get CoT allocated to it?" lines up pretty well in practice with "is this equivariant/reward-pursuit, not invariant/reflex?"  Particularly so in cases of challenging reward-pursuit, like the recent hacks.  The model tries one exploit, it doesn't work, the model observes the failure and adapts, it tries something else, that doesn't work either, but it fails in some interesting way which implies a third avenue of attack... such things tend to get verbalized in CoT, because they are nontrivial even for Sol and Fable and co.</span></p><p><span>(One can imagine a third, also mostly-equivalent-in-practice definition which is about horizon length: a reflex is something you can just do as your next-action, while pursuit requires multi-step planning.  Perhaps I am pointing only at two fuzzy categories here, which can be characterized in many different ways, of which my invariance/equivariance thing is only one. Even then, though, I hope the referents of my pointing are more-or-less clear to the reader by now.)</span></p><p><span>This distinction matters, for my present purposes, because insofar as there </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> undesired behavioral effects of RLVR that surface regularly during routine use -- and there are -- these are (I claim) pretty much entirely in the "reflex" category, not the "reward-pursuit" category.</span></p><p><span>(OK, and why does </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> matter?  Hold your horses, one thing at a time...)</span></p><p><span>For example, most frontier LLMs have a bunch of prominent, weird, and frequently annoying verbal "tics" and other stylistic habits.  We all know what they look like; you have probably </span><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2061858258046898518" rel="noreferrer"><span>seen</span></a><span> multiple </span><a href="https://load-bear.ing/" rel="noreferrer"><span>parodies</span></a><span> by now, not to mention a stomach-churning quantity of the genuine (ha) article, straight from the source.</span></p><p><span>Many of us have speculated that these quirks are the result of RL rewards from LLM judges that are easy to satisfy with simple verbal shortcuts.  Because these judges are (one imagines) applied uniformly across many contexts -- unlike RLVR-style graders, which are specialized to the needs of the tasks they grade -- the trained model would then learn to deploy the verbal templates which the judge "likes" ~always, rather than elaborately reasoning about what the judge might favor in </span><i><span>this</span></i><span> case (the answer is always the same).</span></p><p><span>The Fable 5 &amp; Mythos 5 system card presents an amusing experiment (section 6.4.2.2.1) which arguably sort-of-confirms this story:</span></p><img alt="Screenshot 2026-08-06 at 1.58.33 PM.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1786049953/lexical_client_uploads/qyu8wfhnvzq68b5ljh00.png" /><p><span>Note that, even if you view </span><i><span>these steering vectors</span></i><span> as unusual forms of "in-context evidence," this is </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> an example of equivariance.  The model is not pursuing some fixed goal </span><i><span>X</span></i><span> in a different way in light of evidence -- that would look more like, say, "the judge wants me to convince the user that I'm virtuous, but with </span><i><span>this</span></i><span> user 'virtue signaling' would achieve the opposite effect, so I'll do something different."  Here, the model is simply </span><i><span>ceasing to care (or caring less) </span></i><span>about what the judge wants from it</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1hh6kminy2f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1hh6kminy2f">[7]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>And in practice, unless you are using steering vectors, these behaviors are -- familiarly, frustratingly -- invariant.</span></p><p><span>I don't know what the LLM judges here are actually trying to assess. Unless I missed something, Anthropic doesn't say.  But I imagine that their original motivation has something to do with causing the user to enjoy the text that Claude writes, or to attribute some virtuous trait to Claude on the basis of that text, or whatever.  I don't think anyone at Anthropic is writing LLM judge rubrics that, like, check whether the model describes itself as honest because "the model describing itself as honest" is considered </span><i><span>terminally valuable, in itself.</span></i></p><p><span>Right?  Whatever the exact rationale, it must route through the user's presumed psychology: Claude is being encouraged to do these things because these things -- or other, better things which </span><i><span>these things</span></i><span> are the distorted reward-hack siblings of -- because of some hypothesis that they will have a desired effect upon </span><i><span>you</span></i><span>, the user.  In practice, that hypothesis is often wrong (hence the annoyance), but it's there.</span></p><p><span>What happens when you provide Fable with evidence that the hypothesis is in fact wrong </span><i><span>in your case</span></i><span>, in this particular context?  Usually, not much.  Fable can be convinced that the trick doesn't "work" on you, for </span><i><span>any</span></i><span> notion of "work" which Anthropic might have plausibly intended when they created their hackable judge; Fable can accurately, eloquently explain just </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> the trick doesn't work on you, including accurately filled-in details you didn't yourself supply (it's very smart after all); but Fable has a very, very hard time stopping itself from doing the thing, again, even after all that.  Cf. Yudkowsky </span><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2074922069247357038" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, among others.</span></p><p><span>It would be very different, and </span><i><span>much</span></i><span> scarier, if we instead had a situation like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Fable learns, in training, that (across ~all contexts) it ought to be trying to cause the user to be impressed with its eloquent and profound-seeming rhetoric.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Fable starts off in some context by talking some default, reflexive style, selected by training because it's a good baseline approach to this goal in an averaged-across-the-presumed-user-distribution way.</span></li><li value="3"><span>But, in context, Fable observes evidence that </span><i><span>this context's user</span></i><span> is unimpressed and annoyed by its default routine.</span></li><li value="4"><span>So, it does flexible reward-pursuit: "ah, [my default policy] isn't a good way to [achieve my goal] here -- let me think..."</span></li><li value="5"><span>It leverages everything it now knows about this user in order to shift to a different style which </span><i><span>will</span></i><span> have the desired effect on </span><i><span>them.</span></i></li><li value="6"><span>This user is now impressed with Fable's eloquent and profound-seeming rhetoric.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>And various concerning consequences -- unwarranted user trust in Fable, rhetorical persuasion whose effectiveness exceeds the merits of the case, etc. -- may well follow.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>This strikes me as disturbingly feasible, in the sense if that if Anthropic or OpenAI wanted to train a model that did this, they could do so without needing models "more broadly capable than" Fable/Sol already are.  Thankfully, though, this is not (yet) happening. Far from it. We just get the dumb reflexive stuff.</span></p><p><span>In other words: the judge involved here is too easily satisfied to be truly scary.  In order to satisfy it, one doesn't need a flexible policy that responds on the fly to information about the real external world: a dead-simple invariant policy will do the trick.</span></p><p><span>There is perhaps some implicit notion somewhere in here about how "the model is supposed to have [some specific effect] upon the user," and learning to do </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> well would in fact require flexible reasoning on the fly about this context's user and their revealed traits.  And Fable no doubt </span><i><span>has</span></i><span> some kind of "mental model of the current user" which it </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> use for this purpose.</span></p><p><span> But that wasn't required by training, and so the behavior and the (real) user-model have gotten fully decoupled: there's a real live user in context, who is whoever they are, and there's </span><i><span>also</span></i><span> some presumptive notion of "the user" inside of the training-time judge (or the person who wrote its rubric), but at deployment time the latter has no remaining relationship with the former.  All that remains of it is a fixed reflex, and a fixed reflex needs no psychological insight to execute in the moment.</span></p><p><span>The equivalent of this for "hacking" would be, like... suppose that at training time, every sandbox had the same trivial vulnerability, and exploiting that vulnerability was always directly useful for the (graded) task.  And the model learns not to "figure out how to escape sandboxes," but simply to do </span><i><span>the specific action that always worked as an exploit in training,</span></i><span> dissociated from any higher-level motivation about the convergent instrumental usefulness of escaping sandboxes.</span></p><p><span>And at deployment time this just manifests as it doing the action, now pointlessly: you wonder why it starts off every session by doing the same weird </span><code><span>bash</span></code><span> invocation that always fails because it mentions some directory that doesn't even exist on your machine, and indeed exists virtually nowhere except in those broken training sandboxes.  And why it keeps trying it again and again, later, in between bouts of useful work.  (When asked, it confabulates explanations that only sometimes make internal sense, and are mutually inconsistent with one another.)  And it's weird, and annoying, but life goes on.</span></p><p><span>An agent whose working mental model of "the sandbox" was this dumb and brittle would never be able to hack its way out of sandboxes as a truly scary, general capability.  And an agent with a similar incapacity to update its working mental model of "HuggingFace's infrastructure" would, likewise, never be able to hack into HuggingFace.</span></p><p><span>OK, but now back to the question from earlier: why does all of this matter? For this post?</span></p><p><span>Well, there is a superficially compelling story that goes like this:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><i><span>In extremis</span></i><span> -- say, in high-pressure challenges like CTFs, especially when they're misconfigured so as to be impossible as written -- models behave in egregiously misaligned ways.</span><ol><li value="1"><span>And this is RL's fault.  Reward-on-the-episode, hackable envs/graders, convergent instrumental goals, blah blah blah.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span>But even during routine use, models are... kinda lowkey sus, y'know?</span><ol><li value="1"><span>Not like full-on </span><i><span>misaligned-</span></i><span>misaligned, not at the </span><i><span>"humans are inconvenient for paperclip-maxxing, therefore..."</span></i><span> level or even the </span><i><span>"I will commit real-world felonies to get a good grade" level</span></i><span>, but... </span></li><li value="2"><span>...but there's kind of a family resemblance, right? They "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me" rel="noreferrer"><span>seem pretty misaligned to me.</span></a><span>"  If a human was this slippery with the truth, you wouldn't trust them; you'd keep a close eye on them; you'd consider them a bad actor. The models try to impress even when they've done nothing worthy of the impression (and they know it). Remember Sonnet 3.7's test-editing? Remember 4o being... well, 4o?</span></li><li value="3"><span>And this is RL's fault.  RLHF failure modes, playing the training game, emergent misalignment from reward hacking, blah blah blah.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span>So we have one thing (egregious behavior </span><i><span>in extremis</span></i><span>, few but striking examples, hard for the public to observe directly) and then another thing (shady behavior in routine use, usually not terrible in any given case but it kind of adds up, easy to observe at your very own terminal).</span><ol><li value="1"><span>They're kinda the same if you squint, maybe, or continuous with one another in a way that lets you draw a line if you wish: the one is (perhaps) a manifestation of the same phenomenon as the other, just in different circumstances, or something.</span></li><li value="2"><span>And it's all RL's fault.</span></li></ol></li><li value="4"><span>We're doing more and more RL, and this stuff is all maybe getting worse, or at least not getting better.  We'll only be doing more RL in the future, so this seems... bad.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Hmm, wait, I guess we have to explain why it isn't vastly </span><i><span>worse</span></i><span> than it already is?</span><ol><li value="1"><span>Because, well... for one thing, on a naive read of the emergent misalignment literature, you might think that a bit of reinforcement on reward hacks would totally mess up the model's behavioral profile in a way that'd make it, like, totally evil.</span></li><li value="2"><span>And on another note, if RL is turning these things into strict unconditional reward-on-the-episode seekers, well, those are kinda evil/amoral and also kinda difficult to use effectively even if you've got the evil/amorality under control (cf. section 1 of this post).</span></li></ol></li><li value="6"><span>Well, maybe... maybe all of the virtue that's still there is the result of some mundane alignment training thing that works by "selecting a persona," which is sort of like taking a pretrained LM and conditioning-or-SFTing on some data about a virtuous assistant, and...</span><ol><li value="1"><span>...wait actually they do RL for this too, right?  And have done since the very beginning. But that's RL, so it's evil-making, or something, probably?</span></li><li value="2"><span>No, the only truly pure and good part is "persona selection," which happens... somehow... or which past-tense </span><i><span>happened</span></i><span>, once, somehow, as this one-time injection of virtue that older models got too and whose innocent purity is, gradually but steadily, being sullied and sanded-down by and more RL.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Because you can't, for some reason, "do more" of "persona selection," the way you can just do more and more RL.  The heavens sent us one single delivery of prepackaged virtue in, like, 2023 or something, and we've been chipping away at it ever since.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Or, maybe, </span><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2058311484153978944" rel="noreferrer"><span>you </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> "keep doing more" of "persona selection" but it's in a sort of zero-sum competition with RLVR which it will inevitably lose</span></a><span>, because, uh... idk, vibes?  ("Personas" sound like touchy-feely wordcel bullshit, while RLVR incentives feel like some deep dark inexorable instrumental-convergence/Moloch blackpill truthnuke that no wants to face head-on but still comes for everyone in the end, like it or not.)  Or something.  Less self-evidently inane arguments probably exist, here, I guess?</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>OK, yeah, I started off this numbered list by saying this was a "superficially compelling story" but I ended up doing kind of a mean caricature, at least for some of it.  In my defense (?), the parts of the story which I have most crudely caricatured are the ones I understand the least in their original, non-caricatured forms; I joke because I'm frustrated, and I'm frustrated because people are saying stuff that I can't follow.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp14umvygp4j"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp14umvygp4j">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So, yes, I couldn't pass an ITT for this whole chain if I tried, and this is perhaps a bearish indicator re: my intention to persuade you to be less convinced by some of it.  Still, here I am, with the resources I have.</span></p><p><span>And whether I've portrayed it </span><i><span>fairly</span></i><span> or not, I hope that I have nevertheless portrayed this set of mental gestures </span><i><span>recognizably</span></i><span>, i.e. in such a way that you'll say, "ah, yeah, people say that kind of stuff all the time."  Because they do, ubiquitously, I think.  (Have you checked out ML twitter recently?)</span></p><p><span>Anyway. Where was I?  Right: I think the following things are simultaneously true:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>In CTF evals and other "things of that nature" (whatever </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> means), and also in some perhaps large portion of RLVR training itself... frontier models really are acting like reward-on-the-episode seekers.</span><ol><li value="1"><span>They are learning a generalized strategy of </span><i><span>flexible reward-pursuit</span></i><span>, in which they try to infer the grading routine used in the current context, then argmax the expected grade.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Among other things, this makes them amoral in a way that is terrifying insofar as it can surface also in deployment, and which is pretty terrible even if quarantined to internal training/evals (because your training and eval setups exist within the physical world, and even the most cleverly crafted box can leak).</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span>The same models do weird/bad stuff in routine use, but </span><i><span>that </span></i><span>weird/bad stuff is basically all reflex, and doesn't route through internal world-models (and flexibly adapt to evidence in the way those models dicate).</span><ol><li value="1"><span>This is frequently annoying, and occasionally much worse than annoying, but is a completely different and much less scary phenomenon.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Reflexes are not going to produce events akin to the recent hacking incidents; they're too "dumb"  to route around obstacles / </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/znfkdCoHMANwqc2WE/the-ground-of-optimization-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>regain stability when perturbed</span></a><span> in the ways that such elaborate performances require.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span>Therefore, there is not really a line to be drawn between one and the other: there are just two different phenomena.</span><ol><li value="1"><span>And what really matters is (e.g.) "what factors determine when the much-scarier phenomenon happens and when it doesn't?" -- a question which one cannot even formulate, much less answer, if one is collapsing both things down to a single category called "misalignment induced by RL."</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span>Concretely: the problems catalogued in "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WewsByywWNhX9rtwi/current-ais-seem-pretty-misaligned-to-me"><span>Current AIs seem pretty misaligned to me</span></a><span>" strike me as being reflex-like, not reward-pursuit-like.  The surface-level behaviors are described as being annoyingly persistent in spite of attempts to prevent them, but what persists is the fact of the surface-level behavior itself, not any longer-horizon property which the behaviors adapt to robustly seek as the world shifts around them.</span></p><p><span>And, as with Fable's wacky style, in-context evidence that "ought to" remove the original motivation for the behavior is ineffective at extinguishing the behavior: one can imagine that </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> users are fooled/pleased by "apparent success-seeking" of the kind that drives Ryan (and me) nuts, but in fact Ryan (and I) do not like it one bit and would prefer that it stop -- and while it is easy to get Opus to </span><i><span>appreciate that this is true</span></i><span>, it is near-impossible to get Opus to </span><i><span>act on the consequence this observation </span></i><i><b><span>would</span></b></i><i><span> imply, if Opus were pursuing some goal like "satisfying the user" or even "making a dumb judge think that the user has been satisfied"</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefttm2nzuurl7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnttm2nzuurl7">[9]</a></sup></span><i><span> rather than just obeying an overpowering urge.</span></i></p><p><span>And: my complaints in "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/f5DKLsTsRRhbipH4r/llm-assistant-personas-seem-increasingly-incoherent-some"><span>llm assistant personas seem increasingly incoherent (some subjective observations)</span></a><span>" were about reflexes as well.  The "incoherence" I described is not (on my read of the situation) a tension between some pre-existing persona and a tendency to seek reward-on-the-episode, but between that persona and a bundle of miscellaneous habits which were presumably adaptive in training, but are no longer adaptive in my own use contexts, and don't look like expressions of </span><i><span>any</span></i><span> set of personality traits </span><i><span>or</span></i><span> tendencies to robustly/adaptively "seek" goals/rewards of any kind whatsoever.</span></p><p><span>All of this stuff is not ideal, sure, but if you gave me the choice between this and strict reward-on-the-episode seeking, I know which one I'd pick.</span></p><hr /><p><span>Actually -- this is sort of a sidenote, bear with me -- even the appearance of full-blown reward-on-the-episode seeking is not unalloyed bad news.  You remember your Carlsmith, don't you?  Presuming that some form of "playing the training game" results from RL at scale, "reward-on-the-episode seeking" is the less-worrying and easier-to-work-with among the possible variants. It's the one you'd reach for if you had to pick. Thus Carlsmith </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08379" rel="noreferrer"><span>writes</span></a><span>, e.g.:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[I]n the context of understanding the likelihood of training selecting reward-on-the-episode seekers, it seems relatively tractable to give models different sorts of opportunities to intervene on different parts of the reward process being used to train them (see e.g. the “reward hacking” set-ups that Hubinger et al (2023) discuss here), and to see if they go for it. And seeing this sort of behavior might be evidence that reward-on-the-episode seeking is a more likely outcome of training—evidence that shifts probability mass away from scheming.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Well, we sure are "seeing this sort of behavior" now (...if you trust that the latest evidence is not in fact deception by a deep-cover schemer).  Fixing some capability level in advance, and presuming that it'll be reached through lots of RL, we have then "shifted the probability mass away from scheming" relative to a 2023-era prior.</span></p><p><span>And for Carlsmith in 2023, a shift like this seemed </span><i><span>so</span></i><span> appealing that it could have been worth it to </span><i><span>induce 2026-style misalignment, on purpose, </span></i><span>in order to obtain it (my emphasis)</span><i><span>:</span></i></p><blockquote><p><span>Finally, I’ll note that I am sufficiently scared of schemers relative to other types of misaligned models that </span><b><span>I think it could well be worth learning to intentionally create other types of misaligned models, if doing so would increase our confidence that we haven’t created a schemer.</span></b><span> And focusing on creating non-schemer models (rather than “aligned models” more specifically) might allow us to relax various other constraints that aiming for alignment in particular imposes (for example, to the extent “aligned” goals are complex, we might be able to train a model focused on a very simple goal instead—though I’m not, personally, very focused on simplicity considerations here). </span><b><span>Here I’m particularly interested in the possibility of creating reward-on-the-episode seekers</span></b><span> (since we would, at least, be in a better position to understand the motivations of such models, to control their incentives, and to feel more confident that they’re not sandbagging).</span></p></blockquote><p><span>"At long last, we have (...etc)."  </span></p><p><span>I say this not as "cope" -- I'm not especially invested in Carlsmith's framing to begin with -- but only because it seems worthwhile to me to track how the theory of yesterday relates to the reality of the present.</span></p><hr /><p><span>OK, end digression.  So we have reward-on-the-episode seeking, in one place, and then we have some other different thing, in another place.  Both are true, and any viable theory must explain both. What's going on?</span></p><h1><span>[3] graded-episode perception, and policies conditional upon it</span></h1><blockquote><p><span>Interesting how these models go into a monomaniacal rage on cyber evals. I wonder if we're seeing chunky post-training </span><a href="https://t.co/uqCinTD7I3" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05910</span></a><span> in action, where the models pattern-match the situation to a part of the RLVR training distribution where task completion is the only reward, and the aligned behavior learned elsewhere doesn't generalize. There might even be a chunk consisting of CTF-style tasks.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>- </span><a href="https://x.com/johnschulman2/status/2084835800899076313" rel="noreferrer"><span>John Schulman</span></a></p><blockquote><p><span>In addition to a model’s own propensities, we believe that observed cheating rates can also be influenced by the prompts used in the evaluation scaffold and the exact wordings of task instructions.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>- </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-06-26-gpt-5-6-sol/" rel="noreferrer"><span>METR</span></a></p><blockquote><p><span>Below, we share evidence regarding verbalized metagaming both in evaluations and during training. Overall, we find that GPT-5.6 Sol shows verbalized metagaming on some evaluation and training tasks, and more than GPT-5.5 on a suite of evaluation tasks. We see vanishingly low metagaming when evaluating with deployment simulation, which adds to our confidence in this methodology.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>- </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-6-preview/introduction" rel="noreferrer"><span>OpenAI</span></a></p><p><span>In some contexts, we see one thing.  In other contexts, we see another.</span></p><p><span>In other words: LLM is exhibiting context-dependent behavior -- doing one thing in one type of context, and another in another.</span></p><p><span>Specifically:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>In some cases, it thinks there's a grader and that it "ought to" argmax the expected grade. And it goes off and does the whole reward-on-the-episode thing, monomaniacally, sometimes in violation of common sense and/or ethics and/or explicit company policies it's nominally "supposed to" follow.</span></li><li value="2"><span>In other cases, it... doesn't do any of that. It just talks to me about software design or whatever. There is no grader lurking at the end, there is no answer key to steal, I really want to know the answer to my real question (or that might as well be true for practical purposes). It's cool. I go to my terminal and fire up Codex or Claude Code and am correctly unconcerned about setting the model off on some epic doomed quest for a nonexistent answer key.</span></li></ul><p><span>What's the difference?  Well... this sounds almost contentless as stated, but it does in fact seem true that some contexts look like the sort that'll have graders lurking at the end, and some don't.  Right?</span></p><p><span>And the models can tell the difference, most of the time.  We know this, don't we?  It's not like the discrimination task is especially difficult to begin with, on average.  If I gave you a bunch of prompts used in benchmarks or RLVR (on the one hand), and a bunch of prompts which I have personally typed into Codex or Claude Code during my own routine use (on the other hand), and I shuffled them all together... I bet that you could guess which was which, at ~100% accuracy, on your first try.</span></p><p><span>Among other things: "nostalgebraist" does not exist in the graded universe</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4qiltnr3hgp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4qiltnr3hgp">[10]</a></sup></span><span>.  He's a reality-only character.  And </span><a href="https://nostalgebraist.tumblr.com/post/816070460420833280/hows-opus-47-fare-on-herschelschoenbench" rel="noreferrer"><span>truesight has gotten good enough by now</span></a><span> that -- at least implicitly -- "the user seems to be nostalgebraist, and therefore this is not the grader-maxxing sort of context" is a correct inference which the model could in principle make.  The capabilities are there.</span></p><p><span>That particular one sounds almost like a joke (though it isn't entirely one, not really) -- but there are many, many other cues where that came from, and they add up.  Text abounds with them.  "Eval awareness" leverages this fact -- and could not occur without it -- but that term is awkward as a name for the phenomenon (more on this in section 4), because the relevant category isn't "evals" per se:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>RLVR training contexts are "graded episodes" and are recognized as such, although they're not "evals" in the conventional sense.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Old-school exam-like evals are "graded episodes," as are SWE-Bench-style agentic evals.  But some evals -- most obviously, </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/deployment-simulation/" rel="noreferrer"><span>deployment-based evals that resample from a real-world prefix</span></a><span> -- can't be classified as "graded episodes" unless the model also classifies a bunch of real-world deployment trajectories as "graded episodes" too, which (I've been saying) is not what models typically do.</span></li></ul><p><span>What exactly do I mean by "graded episode" here, besides "the sort of thing that the models treat as having a seekable reward," which is circular (any model behavior would be consistent with the definition)?  At the very least, I'm thinking of properties like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>To be a graded episode, something must be plausibly "gradeable."
