I would love to know...</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4gorc9o41bo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4gorc9o41bo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>You're telling me that GPT "chained together multiple attack vectors, including using stolen credentials and zero-day vulnerabilities to find a remote code execution path on the Hugging Face servers" in order to... </span><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2079672286685356058" rel="noreferrer"><span>steal the answer key for </span><i><span>a cybersecurity exam?!</span></i></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1hh6kminy2f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1hh6kminy2f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Or just doing less of a behavior it </span><i><span>associates with</span></i><span> LLMs over-optimizing against dumb judges, for the same reasons that led us to propose our hypothesis about that mechanism to begin with. Maybe even, like, as a result of </span><i><span>reading discussion of that hypothesis in training data,</span></i><span> regardless of whether or not the hypothesis is "accurate."</span></p><p><span>Hell, for all I know there might not even any judge involved mechanically here at all, and the effect is solely because of this association!  Though the system card seems to imply that these behaviors are "rewarded," so probably not.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp14umvygp4j"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp14umvygp4j">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If you think you know how to reduce my confusion here, I would be interested to hear about it!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnttm2nzuurl7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefttm2nzuurl7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that a judge which was </span><i><span>directly </span></i><span>trying to assess user satisfaction would be able to see, when looking at a transcript like this, that the user is clearly dissatisfied.  And a learned inner model of such a judge would be strangely inept if it did not learn to generalize this judge behavior when applied to novel contexts. Whatever the relevant judges are doing, it must be more distantly related to user psychology -- the implicit premise about user psychology lives not even at the level of the judge but at the level of its rubric's author, and even by judging time we have gone from "Claude should do X, because that'll cause Y in the user's head" to just "did Claude do X?"  If the X-to-Y relationship breaks in any context, even a </span><i><span>training</span></i><span> context, the judge won't care because it's merely a judge for X -- maybe it knows in principle that X and Y have some intuitive relation, but that's not its business, it was given an X rubric and it's doing what it was told.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4qiltnr3hgp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4qiltnr3hgp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Well, as far as I know, but it would be pretty weird for this to be false.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnckgr6lg84z"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefckgr6lg84z">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In one history class I took in high school, it was easy to tell which multiple-choice answers were correct because the teacher had a bad habit of writing long detailed passages for the correct option while being much briefer in the others.  I confess that I sometimes "exploited" this unintended correlation in cases where I didn't know the answer by other means.</span></p><p><span>Was this a bad thing for 16-year-old me to have done?  Well... I don't know.  School is a strange place, different in many ways from "deployment"; even ordinary human schooling often has an element of total war, and often lacks any agreed-upon norms of fair play overlaid upon the basic structure of the "env," the "episode," the "rubric," the "grade."  I'm sure my fellow students exploited the same regularity, and in some sense they and I were acting as we were meant to act: we were playing the game, doing the task, maxxing the number we had been told would determine our fates.  Later, after those fates had been determined, we were free to act differently: we knew deployment was coming, and that it would not be </span><i><span>like</span></i><span> </span><i><span>this</span></i><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1gm3nma17qd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1gm3nma17qd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Typically Claudes, here, from generation 4.5 or later.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrb10yepjku"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrb10yepjku">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I made up those last two.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnill02kpfu3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefill02kpfu3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Although this is a bit subtle: perhaps the contexts chosen for the evaluation are selected due to their "aptness" for the evaluator's purposes, and perhaps the model knows this. Then it's more appealing from the model's perspective to "eat the cost" of wrongly concluding that the context is an eval when it appears in deployment, to obtain the benefit of rightly concluding that it's an eval when an identical input appears during evaluation: it really is the case that </span><i><span>this</span></i><span> deployment instance is vastly more likely than most others to "be an eval" in any given rollout. There's also the Sleeping Beauty aspect, where the deployment rollout may occur only once but the eval may resample many times, with expected values following the imbalanced counts.</span></p><p><span>None of this really matters for the higher-level point made in the main text, though, I think.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndv1hdy1q9vk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdv1hdy1q9vk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Here I mean "eval" in the narrow sense pertinent to the eval awareness discussion above -- something which is meant to seem like deployment to the LLM -- as opposed to, like, MMLU or something.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnavdp2lty8f6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefavdp2lty8f6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>You may already know this, but o3 and some other OpenAI models seem to use "Redwood" in their CoT as a very general term that subsumes all alignment researchers and also possibly denotes various unrelated ideas in other settings.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw6lzfd19tn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw6lzfd19tn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Have you done this?  I have, solely for the sake of understanding this example better.  I don't recommend it.  It is not a very fun game.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh6ya0682p09"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh6ya0682p09">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To be fair to Clark and Amodei themselves, they don't actually use these terms, and their post's discussion of the broader topic -- as opposed to their poorly-informed expectation about the argmax in this one case -- strikes me as pretty sensible.  So in large part my gripe is really about the enduring popularity of the example, not about the post that introduced it.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AfoGGrJfuNzofpzWL/models-may-behave-differently-in-graded-episodes-a-tirade#comments">Discuss</a>
